========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I guess I came in because I haven’t really felt like myself lately. It started after I tried to sort of clean up my image and my routine for this arts board stuff I’m involved with, like trying to be more put together and reliable and professional, I guess. And I thought that would make me feel better, like more in control, but instead I’ve just been more tense and kind of down than I expected.

I’m still doing the basics, like I’m not totally falling apart or anything, but I keep dropping these little things I normally care about. Like fair planning notes, design drafts, small tasks that usually I’d stay on top of or even enjoy. Then I feel really guilty about it, and instead of just catching up or asking for help, I end up canceling plans or pulling back, which just makes me feel worse. So… I’m here because it feels like this reaction is getting in the way more than I want to admit.

Sort of… not as much as I used to, I guess. Like, the arts board and fair planning stuff used to feel exciting to me, or at least satisfying — making notes, working on design drafts, feeling like I was contributing something. But since I started trying to clean up my image and be more organized and professional about it all, it’s like I put so much pressure on myself that those things feel tense instead of enjoyable.

Then I feel guilty, and instead of getting back into it or asking someone to help me catch up, I’ll cancel plans or pull back. So I wouldn’t say I feel no pleasure at all, but it’s definitely dulled, and a lot of things I usually enjoy feel more like obligations right now.

I don’t know if I’d say anxious most of the time, exactly… but I do feel tense a lot more than I expected. It’s like since I started trying to clean up my image and get my routine together for the arts board stuff, I’ve been carrying this pressure around.

I’m still doing basic things, so it’s not like I’m completely unable to function, but I keep messing up or dropping these little things that normally matter to me, like fair planning notes or design drafts. And then I feel really guilty about it, and instead of just catching up or asking someone for help, I cancel plans or kind of pull back. So it’s more like this steady tension and feeling down around all of that, rather than panic or anything.

I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I mean… I do worry sometimes even when things are technically going okay, but I don’t know if I’d say it’s a constant thing or that I’m always waiting for something bad to happen.

It’s more like if something seems to be going smoothly, I’ll still have this little voice in my head like, “Okay, but did I miss something?” or “Am I going to mess this up somehow?” But I don’t know if that’s worry in the way you mean it. Sometimes I can enjoy things being fine, and other times I kind of pick at them in my head until they don’t feel fine anymore. So… yes, maybe a little, but I’m not sure it’s all the time.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… sometimes it does kind of stretch out over a few days, but I’m not sure I’d call it “worrying” in a big dramatic way. It’s more like this pressure just sits there.

Like, since I started trying to clean up my image and get my routine together for the arts board stuff, I’ll get stuck thinking about whether I’m handling things right, whether I’m going to fall behind, whether people are noticing I’m not as on top of things as I want to seem. And then if I drop something small—like fair planning notes or a design draft that I normally would care about and keep track of—I don’t just think, “Okay, fix it.” I feel guilty about it for a while.

So it can last, yeah. I’ll carry it into the next day, and then instead of catching up or asking someone for help, I’ll cancel plans or pull back because I feel embarrassed or like I need to get myself together first. I’m still doing the basic stuff, so it’s not like I’m completely falling apart, but it does kind of linger and take up more space in my head than I want it to.

It’s more like this pressure just sits there.\n\nLike, since I started trying to clean up my image and get my routine together for the arts board stuff, I’ll get stuck thinking about whether I’m handling things right, whether I’m going to fall behind, whether people are noticing I’m not as on top of things as I want to seem. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not really sure. I don’t think I’ve had, like, a clear period before this where I would’ve said, “I’m depressed,” if that makes sense.

I mean, I’ve had bad days, or times where I was upset or discouraged about things, but I’m having trouble separating what’s just normal sadness from something more than that. I don’t remember having a stretch before this current situation where it felt like the same kind of cloud hanging over everything. But I also don’t want to say “never” too confidently, because I don’t always label things that way when I’m in them.

So… as far as I can tell, no, not really. Not in a way that stands out to me.

People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know that it’s “no obvious reason,” exactly. That’s the part I get stuck on, because in my head there’s usually some reason, even if it’s not a very good one or it’s not something other people would think is worth worrying about.

Like, if everything is basically fine, I might still find some small thing to focus on and turn it over too much. But I wouldn’t say I’m just randomly worried out of nowhere all the time. It’s more like I can connect it to something, even if the connection is kind of thin.

So… I’m not sure. I guess I have noticed myself worrying when maybe there isn’t a big reason to, but I don’t know if I’d describe it as happening often. It feels more situational than constant.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess I came in because I haven’t really felt like myself lately. It started after I tried to sort of clean up my image and my routine for this arts board stuff I’m involved with, like trying to be more put together and reliable and professional, I guess. And I thought that would make me feel better, like more in control, but instead I’ve just been more tense and kind of down than I expected.\n\nI’m still doing the basics, like I’m not totally falling apart or anything, but I keep dropping these little things I normally care about. Like fair planning notes, design drafts, small tasks that usually I’d stay on top of or even enjoy. Then I feel really guilty about it, and instead of just catching up or asking for help, I end up canceling plans or pulling back, which just makes me feel worse. So… I’m here because it feels like this reaction is getting in the way more than I want to admit.\n\nDoctor: Do you still find pleasure in activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Sort of… not as much as I used to, I guess. Like, the arts board and fair planning stuff used to feel exciting to me, or at least satisfying — making notes, working on design drafts, feeling like I was contributing something. But since I started trying to clean up my image and be more organized and professional about it all, it’s like I put so much pressure on myself that those things feel tense instead of enjoyable.\n\nI can still do basic things, I’m not completely unable to function, but I keep dropping these smaller tasks I normally would care about. Then I feel guilty, and instead of getting back into it or asking someone to help me catch up, I’ll cancel plans or pull back. So I wouldn’t say I feel no pleasure at all, but it’s definitely dulled, and a lot of things I usually enjoy feel more like obligations right now.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious most of the time?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say anxious most of the time, exactly… but I do feel tense a lot more than I expected. It’s like since I started trying to clean up my image and get my routine together for the arts board stuff, I’ve been carrying this pressure around.\n\nI’m still doing basic things, so it’s not like I’m completely unable to function, but I keep messing up or dropping these little things that normally matter to me, like fair planning notes or design drafts. And then I feel really guilty about it, and instead of just catching up or asking someone for help, I cancel plans or kind of pull back. So it’s more like this steady tension and feeling down around all of that, rather than panic or anything.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost any weight recently?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. I mean, I haven’t been tracking my weight closely or anything, so I can’t give you an exact number, but my clothes don’t feel noticeably different and no one’s mentioned anything. I haven’t had that sense of, like, “Oh, I’m suddenly losing weight.” So… as far as I can tell, no noticeable weight loss recently.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying about things even when they are going well?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I mean… I do worry sometimes even when things are technically going okay, but I don’t know if I’d say it’s a constant thing or that I’m always waiting for something bad to happen.\n\nIt’s more like if something seems to be going smoothly, I’ll still have this little voice in my head like, “Okay, but did I miss something?” or “Am I going to mess this up somehow?” But I don’t know if that’s worry in the way you mean it. Sometimes I can enjoy things being fine, and other times I kind of pick at them in my head until they don’t feel fine anymore. So… yes, maybe a little, but I’m not sure it’s all the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying about things for days at a time?\n\nPatient: Yeah… sometimes it does kind of stretch out over a few days, but I’m not sure I’d call it “worrying” in a big dramatic way. It’s more like this pressure just sits there.\n\nLike, since I started trying to clean up my image and get my routine together for the arts board stuff, I’ll get stuck thinking about whether I’m handling things right, whether I’m going to fall behind, whether people are noticing I’m not as on top of things as I want to seem. And then if I drop something small—like fair planning notes or a design draft that I normally would care about and keep track of—I don’t just think, “Okay, fix it.” I feel guilty about it for a while.\n\nSo it can last, yeah. I’ll carry it into the next day, and then instead of catching up or asking someone for help, I’ll cancel plans or pull back because I feel embarrassed or like I need to get myself together first. I’m still doing the basic stuff, so it’s not like I’m completely falling apart, but it does kind of linger and take up more space in my head than I want it to.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced periods of feeling down or depressed before this current episode?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t think I’ve had, like, a clear period before this where I would’ve said, “I’m depressed,” if that makes sense.\n\nI mean, I’ve had bad days, or times where I was upset or discouraged about things, but I’m having trouble separating what’s just normal sadness from something more than that. I don’t remember having a stretch before this current situation where it felt like the same kind of cloud hanging over everything. But I also don’t want to say “never” too confidently, because I don’t always label things that way when I’m in them.\n\nSo… as far as I can tell, no, not really. Not in a way that stands out to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying about things even when there's no obvious reason to worry?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that it’s “no obvious reason,” exactly. That’s the part I get stuck on, because in my head there’s usually some reason, even if it’s not a very good one or it’s not something other people would think is worth worrying about.\n\nLike, if everything is basically fine, I might still find some small thing to focus on and turn it over too much. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know if I’d say I “usually” expect something to go wrong. That sounds a little stronger than what I notice in myself.

It’s more like… when things are going well, I don’t always fully relax into it. I might have this small part of me that’s kind of checking for what I’m missing, or wondering if I’m about to drop the ball somehow. But I’m not constantly sitting there thinking disaster is definitely coming.

Like, if something is going smoothly, I can enjoy it, at least somewhat. But then sometimes my mind goes, “Okay, but don’t get too comfortable,” or “Make sure you’re not forgetting something.” I don’t know if that counts as expecting something to go wrong, or just being kind of tense and over-aware.

So… I guess not usually, no. Not in a major way. It’s more occasional, and usually tied to whatever I already feel responsible for.

Sometimes I can enjoy things being fine, and other times I kind of pick at them in my head until they don’t feel fine anymore. And then if I drop something small—like fair planning notes or a design draft that I normally would care about and keep track of—I don’t just think, “Okay, fix it.” I feel guilty about it for a while.\n\nSo it can last, yeah. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure I’d describe myself as always on the lookout, no. Like, I don’t feel like I’m scanning the room or constantly expecting danger or anything like that.

I do sometimes get caught up in thinking, “What am I not noticing?” or “Is there something I should be preparing for?” But that feels more like… I don’t know, trying not to be caught off guard, rather than feeling like something bad is definitely out there. And it depends a lot on the situation. If I already feel responsible for something, then I can get more alert to possible problems, even small ones.

But if you mean just in general, when there’s no clear reason to be concerned, I don’t think that’s really a constant thing for me. I might be a little tense sometimes, but not always watching for danger.

It started after I tried to sort of clean up my image and my routine for this arts board stuff I’m involved with, like trying to be more put together and reliable and professional, I guess. And I thought that would make me feel better, like more in control, but instead I’ve just been more tense and kind of down than I expected.\n\nI’m still doing the basics, like I’m not totally falling apart or anything, but I keep dropping these little things I normally care about. Like fair planning notes, design drafts, small tasks that usually I’d stay on top of or even enjoy. Then I feel really guilty about it, and instead of just catching up or asking for help, I end up canceling plans or pulling back, which just makes me feel worse. So… I’m here because it feels like this reaction is getting in the way more than I want to admit.\n\nDoctor: Do you still find pleasure in activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Sort of… not as much as I used to, I guess. Like, the arts board and fair planning stuff used to feel exciting to me, or at least satisfying — making notes, working on design drafts, feeling like I was contributing something. But since I started trying to clean up my image and be more organized and professional about it all, it’s like I put so much pressure on myself that those things feel tense instead of enjoyable.\n\nI can still do basic things, I’m not completely unable to function, but I keep dropping these smaller tasks I normally would care about. Then I feel guilty, and instead of getting back into it or asking someone to help me catch up, I’ll cancel plans or pull back. So I wouldn’t say I feel no pleasure at all, but it’s definitely dulled, and a lot of things I usually enjoy feel more like obligations right now.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious most of the time?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say anxious most of the time, exactly… but I do feel tense a lot more than I expected. It’s like since I started trying to clean up my image and get my routine together for the arts board stuff, I’ve been carrying this pressure around.\n\nI’m still doing basic things, so it’s not like I’m completely unable to function, but I keep messing up or dropping these little things that normally matter to me, like fair planning notes or design drafts. And then I feel really guilty about it, and instead of just catching up or asking someone for help, I cancel plans or kind of pull back. So it’s more like this steady tension and feeling down around all of that, rather than panic or anything.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost any weight recently?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. I mean, I haven’t been tracking my weight closely or anything, so I can’t give you an exact number, but my clothes don’t feel noticeably different and no one’s mentioned anything. I haven’t had that sense of, like, “Oh, I’m suddenly losing weight.” So… as far as I can tell, no noticeable weight loss recently.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying about things even when they are going well?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I mean… I do worry sometimes even when things are technically going okay, but I don’t know if I’d say it’s a constant thing or that I’m always waiting for something bad to happen.\n\nIt’s more like if something seems to be going smoothly, I’ll still have this little voice in my head like, “Okay, but did I miss something?” or “Am I going to mess this up somehow?” But I don’t know if that’s worry in the way you mean it. Sometimes I can enjoy things being fine, and other times I kind of pick at them in my head until they don’t feel fine anymore. So… yes, maybe a little, but I’m not sure it’s all the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying about things for days at a time?\n\nPatient: Yeah… sometimes it does kind of stretch out over a few days, but I’m not sure I’d call it “worrying” in a big dramatic way. It’s more like this pressure just sits there.\n\nLike, since I started trying to clean up my image and get my routine together for the arts board stuff, I’ll get stuck thinking about whether I’m handling things right, whether I’m going to fall behind, whether people are noticing I’m not as on top of things as I want to seem. And then if I drop something small—like fair planning notes or a design draft that I normally would care about and keep track of—I don’t just think, “Okay, fix it.” I feel guilty about it for a while.\n\nSo it can last, yeah. I’ll carry it into the next day, and then instead of catching up or asking someone for help, I’ll cancel plans or pull back because I feel embarrassed or like I need to get myself together first. I’m still doing the basic stuff, so it’s not like I’m completely falling apart, but it does kind of linger and take up more space in my head than I want it to.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced periods of feeling down or depressed before this current episode?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t think I’ve had, like, a clear period before this where I would’ve said, “I’m depressed,” if that makes sense.\n\nI mean, I’ve had bad days, or times where I was upset or discouraged about things, but I’m having trouble separating what’s just normal sadness from something more than that. I don’t remember having a stretch before this current situation where it felt like the same kind of cloud hanging over everything. But I also don’t want to say “never” too confidently, because I don’t always label things that way when I’m in them.\n\nSo… as far as I can tell, no, not really. Not in a way that stands out to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying about things even when there's no obvious reason to worry?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that it’s “no obvious reason,” exactly. That’s the part I get stuck on, because in my head there’s usually some reason, even if it’s not a very good one or it’s not something other people would think is worth worrying about.\n\nLike, if everything is basically fine, I might still find some small thing to focus on and turn it over too much. But I wouldn’t say I’m just randomly worried out of nowhere all the time. It’s more like I can connect it to something, even if the connection is kind of thin.\n\nSo… I’m not sure. I guess I have noticed myself worrying when maybe there isn’t a big reason to, but I don’t know if I’d describe it as happening often. It feels more situational than constant.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually expect something to go wrong even when things are going well?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say I “usually” expect something to go wrong. That sounds a little stronger than what I notice in myself.\n\nIt’s more like… when things are going well, I don’t always fully relax into it. I might have this small part of me that’s kind of checking for what I’m missing, or wondering if I’m about to drop the ball somehow. But I’m not constantly sitting there thinking disaster is definitely coming.\n\nLike, if something is going smoothly, I can enjoy it, at least somewhat. I might be a little tense sometimes, but not always watching for danger.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you find that you worry about things more than other people do?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 23 / not_in_workforce / associates\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m honestly not sure. I mean, compared to some people, maybe I do? But it’s hard for me to judge because I don’t really know what’s going on in other people’s heads.

Sometimes I’ll say something I’ve been thinking about and someone else will be like, “Oh, I wouldn’t have even thought of that,” and then I realize maybe I spent more time on it than most people would. But other times I feel like my worrying is pretty normal, like it’s connected to real-life stuff and not just… random.

So I guess I wouldn’t confidently say, “Yes, I worry way more than other people.” It’s more that I notice I can get stuck on things longer than I probably need to, but I don’t know if that’s more than other people or just how I handle things.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess I came in because I haven’t really felt like myself lately. It started after I tried to sort of clean up my image and my routine for this arts board stuff I’m involved with, like trying to be more put together and reliable and professional, I guess. And I thought that would make me feel better, like more in control, but instead I’ve just been more tense and kind of down than I expected.\n\nI’m still doing the basics, like I’m not totally falling apart or anything, but I keep dropping these little things I normally care about. Like fair planning notes, design drafts, small tasks that usually I’d stay on top of or even enjoy. Then I feel really guilty about it, and instead of just catching up or asking for help, I end up canceling plans or pulling back, which just makes me feel worse. So… I’m here because it feels like this reaction is getting in the way more than I want to admit.\n\nDoctor: Do you still find pleasure in activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Sort of… not as much as I used to, I guess. Like, the arts board and fair planning stuff used to feel exciting to me, or at least satisfying — making notes, working on design drafts, feeling like I was contributing something. But since I started trying to clean up my image and be more organized and professional about it all, it’s like I put so much pressure on myself that those things feel tense instead of enjoyable.\n\nI can still do basic things, I’m not completely unable to function, but I keep dropping these smaller tasks I normally would care about. Then I feel guilty, and instead of getting back into it or asking someone to help me catch up, I’ll cancel plans or pull back. So I wouldn’t say I feel no pleasure at all, but it’s definitely dulled, and a lot of things I usually enjoy feel more like obligations right now.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious most of the time?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say anxious most of the time, exactly… but I do feel tense a lot more than I expected. It’s like since I started trying to clean up my image and get my routine together for the arts board stuff, I’ve been carrying this pressure around.\n\nI’m still doing basic things, so it’s not like I’m completely unable to function, but I keep messing up or dropping these little things that normally matter to me, like fair planning notes or design drafts. And then I feel really guilty about it, and instead of just catching up or asking someone for help, I cancel plans or kind of pull back. So it’s more like this steady tension and feeling down around all of that, rather than panic or anything.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost any weight recently?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. I mean, I haven’t been tracking my weight closely or anything, so I can’t give you an exact number, but my clothes don’t feel noticeably different and no one’s mentioned anything. I haven’t had that sense of, like, “Oh, I’m suddenly losing weight.” So… as far as I can tell, no noticeable weight loss recently.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying about things even when they are going well?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I mean… I do worry sometimes even when things are technically going okay, but I don’t know if I’d say it’s a constant thing or that I’m always waiting for something bad to happen.\n\nIt’s more like if something seems to be going smoothly, I’ll still have this little voice in my head like, “Okay, but did I miss something?” or “Am I going to mess this up somehow?” But I don’t know if that’s worry in the way you mean it. Sometimes I can enjoy things being fine, and other times I kind of pick at them in my head until they don’t feel fine anymore. So… yes, maybe a little, but I’m not sure it’s all the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying about things for days at a time?\n\nPatient: Yeah… sometimes it does kind of stretch out over a few days, but I’m not sure I’d call it “worrying” in a big dramatic way. It’s more like this pressure just sits there.\n\nLike, since I started trying to clean up my image and get my routine together for the arts board stuff, I’ll get stuck thinking about whether I’m handling things right, whether I’m going to fall behind, whether people are noticing I’m not as on top of things as I want to seem. And then if I drop something small—like fair planning notes or a design draft that I normally would care about and keep track of—I don’t just think, “Okay, fix it.” I feel guilty about it for a while.\n\nSo it can last, yeah. I’ll carry it into the next day, and then instead of catching up or asking someone for help, I’ll cancel plans or pull back because I feel embarrassed or like I need to get myself together first. I’m still doing the basic stuff, so it’s not like I’m completely falling apart, but it does kind of linger and take up more space in my head than I want it to.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced periods of feeling down or depressed before this current episode?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t think I’ve had, like, a clear period before this where I would’ve said, “I’m depressed,” if that makes sense.\n\nI mean, I’ve had bad days, or times where I was upset or discouraged about things, but I’m having trouble separating what’s just normal sadness from something more than that. I don’t remember having a stretch before this current situation where it felt like the same kind of cloud hanging over everything. But I also don’t want to say “never” too confidently, because I don’t always label things that way when I’m in them.\n\nSo… as far as I can tell, no, not really. Not in a way that stands out to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying about things even when there's no obvious reason to worry?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that it’s “no obvious reason,” exactly. That’s the part I get stuck on, because in my head there’s usually some reason, even if it’s not a very good one or it’s not something other people would think is worth worrying about.\n\nLike, if everything is basically fine, I might still find some small thing to focus on and turn it over too much. But I wouldn’t say I’m just randomly worried out of nowhere all the time. It’s more like I can connect it to something, even if the connection is kind of thin.\n\nSo… I’m not sure. I guess I have noticed myself worrying when maybe there isn’t a big reason to, but I don’t know if I’d describe it as happening often. It feels more situational than constant.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually expect something to go wrong even when things are going well?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say I “usually” expect something to go wrong. That sounds a little stronger than what I notice in myself.\n\nIt’s more like… when things are going well, I don’t always fully relax into it. I might have this small part of me that’s kind of checking for what I’m missing, or wondering if I’m about to drop the ball somehow. But I’m not constantly sitting there thinking disaster is definitely coming.\n\nLike, if something is going smoothly, I can enjoy it, at least somewhat. But then sometimes my mind goes, “Okay, but don’t get too comfortable,” or “Make sure you’re not forgetting something.” I don’t know if that counts as expecting something to go wrong, or just being kind of tense and over-aware.\n\nSo… I guess not usually, no. Not in a major way. It’s more occasional, and usually tied to whatever I already feel responsible for.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're always on the lookout for potential problems or dangers, even when there's no clear reason to be concerned?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe myself as always on the lookout, no. Like, I don’t feel like I’m scanning the room or constantly expecting danger or anything like that.\n\nI do sometimes get caught up in thinking, “What am I not noticing?” or “Is there something I should be preparing for?” But that feels more like… I don’t know, trying not to be caught off guard, rather than feeling like something bad is definitely out there. And it depends a lot on the situation. If I already feel responsible for something, then I can get more alert to possible problems, even small ones.\n\nBut if you mean just in general, when there’s no clear reason to be concerned, I don’t think that’s really a constant thing for me. I might be a little tense sometimes, but not always watching for danger.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you worry about things more than other people do?\n\nPatient: I’m honestly not sure. I mean, compared to some people, maybe I do? But it’s hard for me to judge because I don’t really know what’s going on in other people’s heads.\n\nSometimes I’ll say something I’ve been thinking about and someone else will be like, “Oh, I wouldn’t have even thought of that,” and then I realize maybe I spent more time on it than most people would. But other times I feel like my worrying is pretty normal, like it’s connected to real-life stuff and not just… random.\n\nSo I guess I wouldn’t confidently say, “Yes, I worry way more than other people.” It’s more that I notice I can get stuck on things longer than I probably need to, but I don’t know if that’s more than other people or just how I handle things.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.   },

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Almost every day… I’m not sure. I think it depends on what you mean by “worry,” because if it’s like a passing thought of, “Oh, I need to handle that,” then yeah, I probably have thoughts like that most days. But I don’t know if I’d count all of that as worrying.

If you mean actually getting stuck on something, turning it over in my head, feeling bothered by it, then I don’t think I’d say it’s almost every day. It happens, but I don’t track it that closely, and some days I’m more just busy or distracted than actively worrying.

So… maybe not almost every day in a strong way. More like it comes and goes, and when there’s something I feel responsible for, I notice it more.

stressor or its consequences\n  manifestation: The whole reset is still going on, so this has not been some separate problem continuing long after the changes ended.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  description: required\n  manifestation: I am still getting through basic errands and commitments, but I have been less reliable in the volunteer and creative roles people usually count on me for, like fair planning and design follow-through.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 2 months\n  manifestation: This has been going on for about two months, since I started trying to overhaul my routine and presentation.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 23 / not_in_workforce / associates\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S081\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're always on the lookout for potential problems or dangers, even when there's no clear reason to be concerned?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe myself as always on the lookout, no. Like, I don’t feel like I’m scanning the room or constantly expecting danger or anything like that.\n\nI do sometimes get caught up in thinking, “What am I not noticing?” or “Is there something I should be preparing for?” But that feels more like… I don’t know, trying not to be caught off guard, rather than feeling like something bad is definitely out there. And it depends a lot on the situation. If I already feel responsible for something, then I can get more alert to possible problems, even small ones.\n\nBut if you mean just in general, when there’s no clear reason to be concerned, I don’t think that’s really a constant thing for me. I might be a little tense sometimes, but not always watching for danger.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you worry about things more than other people do?\n\nPatient: I’m honestly not sure. I mean, compared to some people, maybe I do? But it’s hard for me to judge because I don’t really know what’s going on in other people’s heads.\n\nSometimes I’ll say something I’ve been thinking about and someone else will be like, “Oh, I wouldn’t have even thought of that,” and then I realize maybe I spent more time on it than most people would. But other times I feel like my worrying is pretty normal, like it’s connected to real-life stuff and not just… random.\n\nSo I guess I wouldn’t confidently say, “Yes, I worry way more than other people.” It’s more that I notice I can get stuck on things longer than I probably need to, but I don’t know if that’s more than other people or just how I handle things.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you worry about things almost every day?\n\nPatient: Almost every day… I’m not sure. I think it depends on what you mean by “worry,” because if it’s like a passing thought of, “Oh, I need to handle that,” then yeah, I probably have thoughts like that most days. But I don’t know if I’d count all of that as worrying.\n\nIf you mean actually getting stuck on something, turning it over in my head, feeling bothered by it, then I don’t think I’d say it’s almost every day. It happens, but I don’t track it that closely, and some days I’m more just busy or distracted than actively worrying.\n\nSo… maybe not almost every day in a strong way. More like it comes and goes, and when there’s something I feel responsible for, I notice it more.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you feel like you're worrying about something most of the time, even when there aren't any obvious problems?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, I don’t think it’s most of the time. I mean, I can get into these stretches where something is on my mind more than I want it to be, and then it feels like it keeps popping back up even when I’m trying to do something else. But if there aren’t any obvious problems, I’m not usually just sitting there worrying constantly.

Sometimes I’ll have this vague feeling like, “Am I forgetting something?” or “Should I be doing something?” but it’s not always tied to a real problem, and it’s not like my whole day is taken over by it. It’s more noticeable when there is something specific going on or something I feel responsible for. Otherwise, I can usually go through the day without feeling like I’m worrying the whole time.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess I came in because I haven’t really felt like myself lately. It started after I tried to sort of clean up my image and my routine for this arts board stuff I’m involved with, like trying to be more put together and reliable and professional, I guess. And I thought that would make me feel better, like more in control, but instead I’ve just been more tense and kind of down than I expected.\n\nI’m still doing the basics, like I’m not totally falling apart or anything, but I keep dropping these little things I normally care about. Like fair planning notes, design drafts, small tasks that usually I’d stay on top of or even enjoy. Then I feel really guilty about it, and instead of just catching up or asking for help, I end up canceling plans or pulling back, which just makes me feel worse. So… I’m here because it feels like this reaction is getting in the way more than I want to admit.\n\nDoctor: Do you still find pleasure in activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Sort of… not as much as I used to, I guess. Like, the arts board and fair planning stuff used to feel exciting to me, or at least satisfying — making notes, working on design drafts, feeling like I was contributing something. But since I started trying to clean up my image and be more organized and professional about it all, it’s like I put so much pressure on myself that those things feel tense instead of enjoyable.\n\nI can still do basic things, I’m not completely unable to function, but I keep dropping these smaller tasks I normally would care about. Then I feel guilty, and instead of getting back into it or asking someone to help me catch up, I’ll cancel plans or pull back. So I wouldn’t say I feel no pleasure at all, but it’s definitely dulled, and a lot of things I usually enjoy feel more like obligations right now.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious most of the time?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say anxious most of the time, exactly… but I do feel tense a lot more than I expected. It’s like since I started trying to clean up my image and get my routine together for the arts board stuff, I’ve been carrying this pressure around.\n\nI’m still doing basic things, so it’s not like I’m completely unable to function, but I keep messing up or dropping these little things that normally matter to me, like fair planning notes or design drafts. And then I feel really guilty about it, and instead of just catching up or asking someone for help, I cancel plans or kind of pull back. So it’s more like this steady tension and feeling down around all of that, rather than panic or anything.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost any weight recently?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. I mean, I haven’t been tracking my weight closely or anything, so I can’t give you an exact number, but my clothes don’t feel noticeably different and no one’s mentioned anything. I haven’t had that sense of, like, “Oh, I’m suddenly losing weight.” So… as far as I can tell, no noticeable weight loss recently.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying about things even when they are going well?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I mean… I do worry sometimes even when things are technically going okay, but I don’t know if I’d say it’s a constant thing or that I’m always waiting for something bad to happen.\n\nIt’s more like if something seems to be going smoothly, I’ll still have this little voice in my head like, “Okay, but did I miss something?” or “Am I going to mess this up somehow?” But I don’t know if that’s worry in the way you mean it. Sometimes I can enjoy things being fine, and other times I kind of pick at them in my head until they don’t feel fine anymore. So… yes, maybe a little, but I’m not sure it’s all the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying about things for days at a time?\n\nPatient: Yeah… sometimes it does kind of stretch out over a few days, but I’m not sure I’d call it “worrying” in a big dramatic way. It’s more like this pressure just sits there.\n\nLike, since I started trying to clean up my image and get my routine together for the arts board stuff, I’ll get stuck thinking about whether I’m handling things right, whether I’m going to fall behind, whether people are noticing I’m not as on top of things as I want to seem. And then if I drop something small—like fair planning notes or a design draft that I normally would care about and keep track of—I don’t just think, “Okay, fix it.” I feel guilty about it for a while.\n\nSo it can last, yeah. I’ll carry it into the next day, and then instead of catching up or asking someone for help, I’ll cancel plans or pull back because I feel embarrassed or like I need to get myself together first. I’m still doing the basic stuff, so it’s not like I’m completely falling apart, but it does kind of linger and take up more space in my head than I want it to.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced periods of feeling down or depressed before this current episode?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t think I’ve had, like, a clear period before this where I would’ve said, “I’m depressed,” if that makes sense.\n\nI mean, I’ve had bad days, or times where I was upset or discouraged about things, but I’m having trouble separating what’s just normal sadness from something more than that. I don’t remember having a stretch before this current situation where it felt like the same kind of cloud hanging over everything. But I also don’t want to say “never” too confidently, because I don’t always label things that way when I’m in them.\n\nSo… as far as I can tell, no, not really. Not in a way that stands out to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying about things even when there's no obvious reason to worry?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that it’s “no obvious reason,” exactly. That’s the part I get stuck on, because in my head there’s usually some reason, even if it’s not a very good one or it’s not something other people would think is worth worrying about.\n\nLike, if everything is basically fine, I might still find some small thing to focus on and turn it over too much. But I wouldn’t say I’m just randomly worried out of nowhere all the time. It’s more like I can connect it to something, even if the connection is kind of thin.\n\nSo… I’m not sure. I guess I have noticed myself worrying when maybe there isn’t a big reason to, but I don’t know if I’d describe it as happening often. It feels more situational than constant.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually expect something to go wrong even when things are going well?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say I “usually” expect something to go wrong. That sounds a little stronger than what I notice in myself.\n\nIt’s more like… when things are going well, I don’t always fully relax into it. I might have this small part of me that’s kind of checking for what I’m missing, or wondering if I’m about to drop the ball somehow. But I’m not constantly sitting there thinking disaster is definitely coming.\n\nLike, if something is going smoothly, I can enjoy it, at least somewhat. But then sometimes my mind goes, “Okay, but don’t get too comfortable,” or “Make sure you’re not forgetting something.” I don’t know if that counts as expecting something to go wrong, or just being kind of tense and over-aware.\n\nSo… I guess not usually, no. Not in a major way. It’s more occasional, and usually tied to whatever I already feel responsible for.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're always on the lookout for potential problems or dangers, even when there's no clear reason to be concerned?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe myself as always on the lookout, no. I might have this small part of me that’s kind of checking for what I’m missing, or wondering if I’m about to drop the ball somehow. But I’m not constantly sitting there thinking disaster is definitely coming.\n\nLike, if something is going smoothly, I can enjoy it, at least somewhat. But then sometimes my mind goes, “Okay, but don’t get too comfortable,” or “Make sure you’re not forgetting something.” I don’t know if that counts as expecting something to go wrong, or just being kind of tense and over-aware.\n\nSo… I guess not usually, no. Not in a major way. It’s more occasional, and usually tied to whatever I already feel responsible for.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're always on the lookout for potential problems or dangers, even when there's no clear reason to be concerned?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe myself as always on the lookout, no. Like, I don’t feel like I’m scanning the room or constantly expecting danger or anything like that.\n\nI do sometimes get caught up in thinking, “What am I not noticing?” or “Is there something I should be preparing for?” But that feels more like… I don’t know, trying not to be caught off guard, rather than feeling like something bad is definitely out there. And it depends a lot on the situation. If I already feel responsible for something, then I can get more alert to possible problems, even small ones.\n\nBut if you mean just in general, when there’s no clear reason to be concerned, I don’t think that’s really a constant thing for me. But it’s hard for me to judge because I don’t really know what’s going on in other people’s heads.\n\nSometimes I’ll say something I’ve been thinking about and someone else will be like, “Oh, I wouldn’t have even thought of that,” and then I realize maybe I spent more time on it than most people would. But other times I feel like my worrying is pretty normal, like it’s connected to real-life stuff and not just… random.\n\nSo I guess I wouldn’t confidently say, “Yes, I worry way more than other people.” It’s more that I notice I can get stuck on things longer than I probably need to, but I don’t know if that’s more than other people or just how I handle things.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you worry about things almost every day?\n\nPatient: Almost every day… I’m not sure. I think it depends on what you mean by “worry,” because if it’s like a passing thought of, “Oh, I need to handle that,” then yeah, I probably have thoughts like that most days. But I don’t know if I’d count all of that as worrying.\n\nIf you mean actually getting stuck on something, turning it over in my head, feeling bothered by it, then I don’t think I’d say it’s almost every day. But if there aren’t any obvious problems, I’m not usually just sitting there worrying constantly.\n\nSometimes I’ll have this vague feeling like, “Am I forgetting something?” or “Should I be doing something?” but it’s not always tied to a real problem, and it’s not like my whole day is taken over by it. Yeah… I think it is, but maybe not in the most dramatic way. Like, I’m still functioning day to day. I still get groceries, answer important messages eventually, show up for basic things I need to do, that kind of stuff. It’s not like I’ve completely stopped doing my routine.

Like, if I’m supposed to help with planning something for the fair, or follow through on a design idea, I’ll tell myself I’m going to do it, and then I either put it off or I overthink it so much that I don’t make the progress I normally would. And then I feel bad because those are things I usually care about and people expect me to be reliable with.

So with relationships, it’s not like I’m fighting with everyone or anything, but I can tell it creates this awkwardness in me, because I know I’m not following through the way I want to. I feel guilty, and then sometimes I avoid checking in because I don’t want to explain why I’m behind. So… yes, it’s affecting things, just more in that I’m not as consistent or useful in those roles as I normally am.

That sounds a little stronger than what I notice in myself.\n\nIt’s more like… when things are going well, I don’t always fully relax into it. I might have this small part of me that’s kind of checking for what I’m missing, or wondering if I’m about to drop the ball somehow. But I’m not constantly sitting there thinking disaster is definitely coming.\n\nLike, if something is going smoothly, I can enjoy it, at least somewhat. But then sometimes my mind goes, “Okay, but don’t get too comfortable,” or “Make sure you’re not forgetting something.” I don’t know if that counts as expecting something to go wrong, or just being kind of tense and over-aware.\n\nSo… I guess not usually, no. Not in a major way. It’s more occasional, and usually tied to whatever I already feel responsible for.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're always on the lookout for potential problems or dangers, even when there's no clear reason to be concerned?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe myself as always on the lookout, no. Like, I don’t feel like I’m scanning the room or constantly expecting danger or anything like that.\n\nI do sometimes get caught up in thinking, “What am I not noticing?” or “Is there something I should be preparing for?” But that feels more like… I don’t know, trying not to be caught off guard, rather than feeling like something bad is definitely out there. And it depends a lot on the situation. If I already feel responsible for something, then I can get more alert to possible problems, even small ones.\n\nBut if you mean just in general, when there’s no clear reason to be concerned, I don’t think that’s really a constant thing for me. I might be a little tense sometimes, but not always watching for danger.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you worry about things more than other people do?\n\nPatient: I’m honestly not sure. Yeah… I think so, sort of. Not necessarily the exact same worry every single day for weeks, like one single thought on repeat, but this whole pattern has been going on for about two months now.

It started around when I was trying to overhaul my routine and, I guess, how I present myself — like trying to get more organized, more put-together, more consistent. At first it felt like a positive thing, like, “Okay, I’m going to fix my schedule and look more like I have my life together.” But then it kind of turned into this ongoing thing where I kept worrying about whether I was doing it right, whether I was falling behind, whether I looked irresponsible or like I wasn’t improving fast enough.

So… yes, in the sense that the worrying has been lasting for weeks at a time. It’s been roughly two months of it coming up again and again since I started trying to make all those changes.

So it’s more like this steady tension and feeling down around all of that, rather than panic or anything.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost any weight recently?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. I mean, I haven’t been tracking my weight closely or anything, so I can’t give you an exact number, but my clothes don’t feel noticeably different and no one’s mentioned anything. I haven’t had that sense of, like, “Oh, I’m suddenly losing weight.” So… as far as I can tell, no noticeable weight loss recently.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying about things even when they are going well?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I mean… I do worry sometimes even when things are technically going okay, but I don’t know if I’d say it’s a constant thing or that I’m always waiting for something bad to happen.\n\nIt’s more like if something seems to be going smoothly, I’ll still have this little voice in my head like, “Okay, but did I miss something?” or “Am I going to mess this up somehow?” But I don’t know if that’s worry in the way you mean it. Sometimes I can enjoy things being fine, and other times I kind of pick at them in my head until they don’t feel fine anymore. So… yes, maybe a little, but I’m not sure it’s all the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying about things for days at a time?\n\nPatient: Yeah… sometimes it does kind of stretch out over a few days, but I’m not sure I’d call it “worrying” in a big dramatic way. It’s more like this pressure just sits there.\n\nLike, since I started trying to clean up my image and get my routine together for the arts board stuff, I’ll get stuck thinking about whether I’m handling things right, whether I’m going to fall behind, whether people are noticing I’m not as on top of things as I want to seem. And then if I drop something small—like fair planning notes or a design draft that I normally would care about and keep track of—I don’t just think, “Okay, fix it.” I feel guilty about it for a while.\n\nSo it can last, yeah. I’ll carry it into the next day, and then instead of catching up or asking someone for help, I’ll cancel plans or pull back because I feel embarrassed or like I need to get myself together first. I’m still doing the basic stuff, so it’s not like I’m completely falling apart, but it does kind of linger and take up more space in my head than I want it to.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced periods of feeling down or depressed before this current episode?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t think I’ve had, like, a clear period before this where I would’ve said, “I’m depressed,” if that makes sense.\n\nI mean, I’ve had bad days, or times where I was upset or discouraged about things, but I’m having trouble separating what’s just normal sadness from something more than that. I don’t remember having a stretch before this current situation where it felt like the same kind of cloud hanging over everything. But I also don’t want to say “never” too confidently, because I don’t always label things that way when I’m in them.\n\nSo… as far as I can tell, no, not really. Not in a way that stands out to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying about things even when there's no obvious reason to worry?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that it’s “no obvious reason,” exactly. That’s the part I get stuck on, because in my head there’s usually some reason, even if it’s not a very good one or it’s not something other people would think is worth worrying about.\n\nLike, if everything is basically fine, I might still find some small thing to focus on and turn it over too much. But I wouldn’t say I’m just randomly worried out of nowhere all the time. It’s more like I can connect it to something, even if the connection is kind of thin.\n\nSo… I’m not sure. I guess I have noticed myself worrying when maybe there isn’t a big reason to, but I don’t know if I’d describe it as happening often. It feels more situational than constant.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually expect something to go wrong even when things are going well?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say I “usually” expect something to go wrong. That sounds a little stronger than what I notice in myself.\n\nIt’s more like… when things are going well, I don’t always fully relax into it. I might have this small part of me that’s kind of checking for what I’m missing, or wondering if I’m about to drop the ball somehow. But I’m not constantly sitting there thinking disaster is definitely coming.\n\nLike, if something is going smoothly, I can enjoy it, at least somewhat. But then sometimes my mind goes, “Okay, but don’t get too comfortable,” or “Make sure you’re not forgetting something.” I don’t know if that counts as expecting something to go wrong, or just being kind of tense and over-aware.\n\nSo… I guess not usually, no. Not in a major way. It’s more occasional, and usually tied to whatever I already feel responsible for.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're always on the lookout for potential problems or dangers, even when there's no clear reason to be concerned?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe myself as always on the lookout, no. Like, I don’t feel like I’m scanning the room or constantly expecting danger or anything like that.\n\nI do sometimes get caught up in thinking, “What am I not noticing?” or “Is there something I should be preparing for?” But that feels more like… I don’t know, trying not to be caught off guard, rather than feeling like something bad is definitely out there. And it depends a lot on the situation. If I already feel responsible for something, then I can get more alert to possible problems, even small ones.\n\nBut if you mean just in general, when there’s no clear reason to be concerned, I don’t think that’s really a constant thing for me. I might be a little tense sometimes, but not always watching for danger.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you worry about things more than other people do?\n\nPatient: I’m honestly not sure. I mean, compared to some people, maybe I do? But it’s hard for me to judge because I don’t really know what’s going on in other people’s heads.\n\nSometimes I’ll say something I’ve been thinking about and someone else will be like, “Oh, I wouldn’t have even thought of that,” and then I realize maybe I spent more time on it than most people would. But other times I feel like my worrying is pretty normal, like it’s connected to real-life stuff and not just… random.\n\nSo I guess I wouldn’t confidently say, “Yes, I worry way more than other people.” It’s more that I notice I can get stuck on things longer than I probably need to, but I don’t know if that’s more than other people or just how I handle things.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you worry about things almost every day?\n\nPatient: Almost every day… I’m not sure. I think it depends on what you mean by “worry,” because if it’s like a passing thought of, “Oh, I need to handle that,” then yeah, I probably have thoughts like that most days. But I don’t know if I’d count all of that as worrying.\n\nIf you mean actually getting stuck on something, turning it over in my head, feeling bothered by it, then I don’t think I’d say it’s almost every day. It happens, but I don’t track it that closely, and some days I’m more just busy or distracted than actively worrying.\n\nSo… maybe not almost every day in a strong way. More like it comes and goes, and when there’s something I feel responsible for, I notice it more.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're worrying about something most of the time, even when there aren't any obvious problems?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think it’s most of the time. I mean, I can get into these stretches where something is on my mind more than I want it to be, and then it feels like it keeps popping back up even when I’m trying to do something else. But if there aren’t any obvious problems, I’m not usually just sitting there worrying constantly.\n\nSometimes I’ll have this vague feeling like, “Am I forgetting something?” or “Should I be doing something?” but it’s not always tied to a real problem, and it’s not like my whole day is taken over by it. It’s more noticeable when there is something specific going on or something I feel responsible for. Otherwise, I can usually go through the day without feeling like I’m worrying the whole time.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your tension and guilt are affecting your daily routine and relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it is, but maybe not in the most dramatic way. Like, I’m still functioning day to day. I still get groceries, answer important messages eventually, show up for basic things I need to do, that kind of stuff. It’s not like I’ve completely stopped doing my routine.\n\nBut I have noticed I’m less dependable in the areas where people usually kind of count on me to be on top of things. Especially with volunteer stuff or creative responsibilities. Like, if I’m supposed to help with planning something for the fair, or follow through on a design idea, I’ll tell myself I’m going to do it, and then I either put it off or I overthink it so much that I don’t make the progress I normally would. And then I feel bad because those are things I usually care about and people expect me to be reliable with.\n\nSo with relationships, it’s not like I’m fighting with everyone or anything, but I can tell it creates this awkwardness in me, because I know I’m not following through the way I want to. I feel guilty, and then sometimes I avoid checking in because I don’t want to explain why I’m behind. So… yes, it’s affecting things, just more in that I’m not as consistent or useful in those roles as I normally am.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying about things for weeks at a time?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so, sort of. Not necessarily the exact same worry every single day for weeks, like one single thought on repeat, but this whole pattern has been going on for about two months now.\n\nIt started around when I was trying to overhaul my routine and, I guess, how I present myself — like trying to get more organized, more put-together, more consistent. At first it felt like a positive thing, like, “Okay, I’m going to fix my schedule and look more like I have my life together.” But then it kind of turned into this ongoing thing where I kept worrying about whether I was doing it right, whether I was falling behind, whether I looked irresponsible or like I wasn’t improving fast enough.\n\nSo… yes, in the sense that the worrying has been lasting for weeks at a time. It’s been roughly two months of it coming up again and again since I started trying to make all those changes.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

But if there aren’t any obvious problems, I’m not usually just sitting there worrying constantly.\n\nSometimes I’ll have this vague feeling like, “Am I forgetting something?” or “Should I be doing something?” but it’s not always tied to a real problem, and it’s not like my whole day is taken over by it. Like, I’m still functioning day to day. I still get groceries, answer important messages eventually, show up for basic things I need to do, that kind of stuff.     "role": "user",

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure I’d say a wide range, exactly. It doesn’t feel like I’m worrying about every possible category of my life all the time, if that makes sense. It’s more like I get stuck on certain things that feel connected, and then my mind kind of branches off from there.

Like, if I’m worried about whether I’m keeping up with something, then it can turn into worrying about how I’m coming across, or whether people are disappointed, or whether I’m going to mess up some responsibility. So it can feel like a lot in the moment, but I don’t know if it’s truly a wide range of things or just the same general kind of worry wearing different outfits.

I guess I’d say I haven’t really noticed myself worrying about everything. It’s more specific than that, even if it gets tangled and takes up more space than I want it to.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess I came in because I haven’t really felt like myself lately. It started after I tried to sort of clean up my image and my routine for this arts board stuff I’m involved with, like trying to be more put together and reliable and professional, I guess. And I thought that would make me feel better, like more in control, but instead I’ve just been more tense and kind of down than I expected.\n\nI’m still doing the basics, like I’m not totally falling apart or anything, but I keep dropping these little things I normally care about. Like fair planning notes, design drafts, small tasks that usually I’d stay on top of or even enjoy. Then I feel really guilty about it, and instead of just catching up or asking for help, I end up canceling plans or pulling back, which just makes me feel worse. Like, the arts board and fair planning stuff used to feel exciting to me, or at least satisfying — making notes, working on design drafts, feeling like I was contributing something. But since I started trying to clean up my image and be more organized and professional about it all, it’s like I put so much pressure on myself that those things feel tense instead of enjoyable.\n\nI can still do basic things, I’m not completely unable to function, but I keep dropping these smaller tasks I normally would care about. Then I feel guilty, and instead of getting back into it or asking someone to help me catch up, I’ll cancel plans or pull back. So I wouldn’t say I feel no pleasure at all, but it’s definitely dulled, and a lot of things I usually enjoy feel more like obligations right now.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious most of the time?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say anxious most of the time, exactly… but I do feel tense a lot more than I expected. It’s like since I started trying to clean up my image and get my routine together for the arts board stuff, I’ve been carrying this pressure around.\n\nI’m still doing basic things, so it’s not like I’m completely unable to function, but I keep messing up or dropping these little things that normally matter to me, like fair planning notes or design drafts. And then I feel really guilty about it, and instead of just catching up or asking someone for help, I cancel plans or kind of pull back. So it’s more like this steady tension and feeling down around all of that, rather than panic or anything.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost any weight recently?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. I mean, I haven’t been tracking my weight closely or anything, so I can’t give you an exact number, but my clothes don’t feel noticeably different and no one’s mentioned anything. I haven’t had that sense of, like, “Oh, I’m suddenly losing weight.” So… as far as I can tell, no noticeable weight loss recently.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying about things even when they are going well?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I mean… I do worry sometimes even when things are technically going okay, but I don’t know if I’d say it’s a constant thing or that I’m always waiting for something bad to happen.\n\nIt’s more like if something seems to be going smoothly, I’ll still have this little voice in my head like, “Okay, but did I miss something?” or “Am I going to mess this up somehow?” But I don’t know if that’s worry in the way you mean it. Sometimes I can enjoy things being fine, and other times I kind of pick at them in my head until they don’t feel fine anymore. So… yes, maybe a little, but I’m not sure it’s all the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying about things for days at a time?\n\nPatient: Yeah… sometimes it does kind of stretch out over a few days, but I’m not sure I’d call it “worrying” in a big dramatic way. It’s more like this pressure just sits there.\n\nLike, since I started trying to clean up my image and get my routine together for the arts board stuff, I’ll get stuck thinking about whether I’m handling things right, whether I’m going to fall behind, whether people are noticing I’m not as on top of things as I want to seem. And then if I drop something small—like fair planning notes or a design draft that I normally would care about and keep track of—I don’t just think, “Okay, fix it.” I feel guilty about it for a while.\n\nSo it can last, yeah. I’ll carry it into the next day, and then instead of catching up or asking someone for help, I’ll cancel plans or pull back because I feel embarrassed or like I need to get myself together first. I’m still doing the basic stuff, so it’s not like I’m completely falling apart, but it does kind of linger and take up more space in my head than I want it to.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced periods of feeling down or depressed before this current episode?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t think I’ve had, like, a clear period before this where I would’ve said, “I’m depressed,” if that makes sense.\n\nI mean, I’ve had bad days, or times where I was upset or discouraged about things, but I’m having trouble separating what’s just normal sadness from something more than that. I don’t remember having a stretch before this current situation where it felt like the same kind of cloud hanging over everything. But I also don’t want to say “never” too confidently, because I don’t always label things that way when I’m in them.\n\nSo… as far as I can tell, no, not really. Not in a way that stands out to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying about things even when there's no obvious reason to worry?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that it’s “no obvious reason,” exactly. That’s the part I get stuck on, because in my head there’s usually some reason, even if it’s not a very good one or it’s not something other people would think is worth worrying about.\n\nLike, if everything is basically fine, I might still find some small thing to focus on and turn it over too much. But I wouldn’t say I’m just randomly worried out of nowhere all the time. It’s more like I can connect it to something, even if the connection is kind of thin.\n\nSo… I’m not sure. I guess I have noticed myself worrying when maybe there isn’t a big reason to, but I don’t know if I’d describe it as happening often. It feels more situational than constant.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually expect something to go wrong even when things are going well?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say I “usually” expect something to go wrong. That sounds a little stronger than what I notice in myself.\n\nIt’s more like… when things are going well, I don’t always fully relax into it. I might have this small part of me that’s kind of checking for what I’m missing, or wondering if I’m about to drop the ball somehow. But I’m not constantly sitting there thinking disaster is definitely coming.\n\nLike, if something is going smoothly, I can enjoy it, at least somewhat. But then sometimes my mind goes, “Okay, but don’t get too comfortable,” or “Make sure you’re not forgetting something.” I don’t know if that counts as expecting something to go wrong, or just being kind of tense and over-aware.\n\nSo… I guess not usually, no. Not in a major way. It’s more occasional, and usually tied to whatever I already feel responsible for.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're always on the lookout for potential problems or dangers, even when there's no clear reason to be concerned?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe myself as always on the lookout, no. Like, I don’t feel like I’m scanning the room or constantly expecting danger or anything like that.\n\nI do sometimes get caught up in thinking, “What am I not noticing?” or “Is there something I should be preparing for?” But that feels more like… I don’t know, trying not to be caught off guard, rather than feeling like something bad is definitely out there. And it depends a lot on the situation. If I already feel responsible for something, then I can get more alert to possible problems, even small ones.\n\nBut if you mean just in general, when there’s no clear reason to be concerned, I don’t think that’s really a constant thing for me. I might be a little tense sometimes, but not always watching for danger.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you worry about things more than other people do?\n\nPatient: I’m honestly not sure. I mean, compared to some people, maybe I do? But it’s hard for me to judge because I don’t really know what’s going on in other people’s heads.\n\nSometimes I’ll say something I’ve been thinking about and someone else will be like, “Oh, I wouldn’t have even thought of that,” and then I realize maybe I spent more time on it than most people would. But other times I feel like my worrying is pretty normal, like it’s connected to real-life stuff and not just… random.\n\nSo I guess I wouldn’t confidently say, “Yes, I worry way more than other people.” It’s more that I notice I can get stuck on things longer than I probably need to, but I don’t know if that’s more than other people or just how I handle things.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you worry about things almost every day?\n\nPatient: Almost every day… I’m not sure. I think it depends on what you mean by “worry,” because if it’s like a passing thought of, “Oh, I need to handle that,” then yeah, I probably have thoughts like that most days. But I don’t know if I’d count all of that as worrying.\n\nIf you mean actually getting stuck on something, turning it over in my head, feeling bothered by it, then I don’t think I’d say it’s almost every day. It happens, but I don’t track it that closely, and some days I’m more just busy or distracted than actively worrying.\n\nSo… maybe not almost every day in a strong way. More like it comes and goes, and when there’s something I feel responsible for, I notice it more.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're worrying about something most of the time, even when there aren't any obvious problems?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think it’s most of the time.
